#7001. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Microsoft is expanding the scope of its AI-powered Project Perception security platform to address the growing concern that AI agents may have access to sensitive information and systems, yet don't always behave as expected.

The artificial intelligence industry is expected to generate an extraordinary $2.67 trillion in worldwide spending this year, according to a new forecast from Gartner.

While security teams have invested heavily in e-mail protection, endpoint security, and identity controls, new research from Fortra suggests one challenge remains difficult to solve: users.

District and state policies run the gamut from embracing to banning AI. This raises a key question: When does AI accelerate student learning and when does it interfere?

#6999. There’s Science Behind Why You’re So Obsessed With Your Partner's Smell

Is it pheromones, chemistry, or something else entirely?

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

You know that thing where your partner's T-shirt somehow smells better after they've worn it? Or when you find yourself nuzzling into their neck during a hug because you genuinely can't get enough of how they smell? It's not necessarily their cologne, shampoo, or laundry detergent you're into, either. Sometimes, it's just them. And apparently, you're really into it.

If you've ever wondered why you're so attracted to your partner's natural smell, you've probably heard that pheromones are to blame. There's this idea that our bodies can somehow sniff out the people we're most compatible with, even before our brains catch up. But the science behind that theory isn't nearly as straightforward as you might think.

“Researchers haven't definitively identified a human sex pheromone that reliably produces sexual attraction in the way we sometimes see in other species,” explains Casey Tanner, a LELO sexpert and certified sex therapist.

That doesn't mean your partner's smell has nothing to do with your feelings for them, though. Our sense of smell is closely connected to memory and emotion, which helps explain why someone's scent can become so comforting (or downright irresistible) once we're attached to them. But does loving the way your partner smells actually mean you're compatible? And should you be concerned if you don't particularly care for their scent? Here's what the science says.

Pheromones are chemical signals released by one member of a species that can trigger specific behavioral or physical responses in another member of the same species. In the animal kingdom, they play a role in everything from attracting mates to marking territory. But when it comes to humans, things get a little less clear.

According to Tanner, while pheromones are well-documented in other animals, their role in human sexual attraction remains unproven. So, there's no solid scientific evidence that your body is sending out some irresistible chemical signal that makes certain people want to jump your bones.

And while pheromones and body odor often get lumped together, they're not actually the same thing. According to Tanner, your natural body odor is essentially your unique scent signature, shaped by everything from genetics and hormones to skin bacteria, diet, and your environment. Unlike pheromones, which are defined by the biological responses they trigger, ordinary body odor doesn't have to produce a specific response to influence how someone feels about you. So, while you might find someone's body odor incredibly attractive, that doesn't mean you're responding to pheromones.

Research suggests that body odor can influence attraction and who we're drawn to, but scientists are still figuring out exactly how much of a role it plays. And just because someone's natural scent appeals to you doesn't mean your bodies have identified each other as a perfect genetic match.

If you've ever found yourself lingering over your partner's pillow after they've left, there's a reason their scent can feel so comforting. And it has a lot to do with how closely our sense of smell is connected to memory and emotion.

“When we're falling for someone, their smell can become intertwined with experiences of pleasure, affection, comfort, and arousal,” Tanner explains. “Over time, that scent can become a kind of shortcut to feeling close to that person!”

Basically, your brain starts associating your partner's natural scent with all the things you love about being around them, from cuddling on the couch to having sex. And those associations can stick around even when you're physically apart.

“Once we’re attached to that person, their body odor essentially becomes a reinforcement or reward,” says psychologist Pamela Dalton, PhD, MPH. Their scent can bring back positive memories and feelings of intimacy, which helps explain why something as simple as a worn shirt can make you feel closer to someone you're missing.

As much as we'd love to tell you that being obsessed with your partner's natural scent means you've found your soulmate, science doesn't exactly back that up. Sure, there may be some biological factors at play, but liking the way someone smells isn't proof that you're genetically compatible, much less destined to spend your lives together.

Some research has explored whether people are more attracted to the body odor of potential partners whose immune-system genes differ from their own. The theory is that these differences could lead to greater immune diversity in biological offspring. But according to Tanner, the findings have been inconsistent, and there's no reliable evidence that humans choose romantic partners based on genetic compatibility.

And even if someone's scent makes you want to bury your face in their chest 24/7, that doesn't tell you much about whether you'll actually work as a couple.

“Loving your partner's scent can be a true reflection of attraction and attachment, but no, it’s not a reliable diagnostic tool for long-term compatibility,” Tanner says. “Chemistry is a great indicator of initial attraction, but compatibility is something we have to discover, negotiate, and create over time.”

In other words, enjoying someone's natural smell is a nice bonus, but it's not a substitute for shared values, trust, communication, or the other things that make a relationship work. And if you're not immediately drawn to someone's scent, that doesn't mean attraction can't develop as you get to know them.

If you've made it this far and realized you've never once felt the urge to steal your partner's T-shirt just to smell it, don't stress. Not everyone is particularly sensitive to scent, and being indifferent to your partner's natural smell doesn't mean you're any less attracted to them.

According to Tanner, people vary in how sensitive they are to smells, how much attention they pay to them, and how important scent is to their experience of attraction. Some people might be more drawn to their partner's appearance, voice, personality, or the emotional connection they share.

“If you’re indifferent to a person’s smell, it’s likely not going to be a game-changer because once you have the opportunity to get to know the person, you find [other] positives and compatibility between the two of you,” says Dalton.

And if you're otherwise happy with your relationship, there's no reason to start questioning it just because your partner's scent doesn't do anything special for you. As Tanner puts it, “If you’re into them, don’t go looking for a problem just because you haven’t noticed their scent!”

Okay, but what if you used to love your partner's natural scent and now you can't stand it? Before you start questioning your entire relationship, know that a change in how you perceive someone's smell doesn't automatically mean your feelings for them have changed.

According to Tanner, everything from hormonal fluctuations and medications to diet, illness, and changes in your sense of smell can affect how you experience your partner's scent. Pregnancy, for example, can make you more sensitive to certain smells. And sometimes, the explanation is as simple as your partner switching shampoos or laundry detergents.

Of course, emotions can play a role, too. Because scent is so closely connected to memory and feelings, your associations with someone's smell can change as your relationship evolves.

“There is evidence that when, for whatever reason, that relationship isn’t working, that body odor is no longer pleasant or preferred,” says Dalton. “All of a sudden, because of the association with negative characteristics, [the body odor] is changing. Your response is changing.”

Still, suddenly disliking your partner's scent isn't proof that something is wrong between you. As Tanner explains, a change in smell isn't a reliable indicator of relationship conflict, and there may be a physical explanation worth considering first.

If you notice a significant change in your partner's actual body odor, it's worth gently asking whether anything has changed with their health, medications, or routine. And if there's a particularly noticeable change in genital odor, Tanner recommends encouraging them to check in with a healthcare professional.

Bottom line: A change in how your partner smells to you isn't automatically a sign that something's wrong. How you feel about your partner's scent can change for all sorts of reasons, and it's just one small part of the bigger picture.

#6998. All of Taylor Swift’s Looks From Her Grand Return to the CMAs

Taylor Swift has returned to the Country Music Awards! Last night, the country singer-turned-global superstar, who recently celebrated the 20th anniversary of her debut, self-titled album, went back to her roots with an appearance (and performance) at the 2026 award show.

The Grammy-winning artist was nominated for Single of the Year for her Toy Story 5 track “I Knew It, I Knew You,” and making her grand entrance, she brought a bit of the franchise’s signature yellow to the red carpet.

Her daffodil gown, with its strapless silhouette and voluminous bubble skirt, was courtesy of Oscar de la Renta, a house Swift has long turned to over the years. After the designer passed away in 2014, she called him her “all-time favorite designer” on social media, and attended the February 2015 New York Fashion Week show to honor his legacy. Over a decade later, she turned to his namesake’s Spring/Summer 2027 collection, and wore her straight-off-the-runway dress with Lorraine Schwartz jewels and Jimmy Choo heels.

For the performance—an Eras Tour-style mashup of “I Knew It, I Knew You” and 2006’s “Our Song”—Swift changed into an ensemble that updated the classic sundress-and-boots uniform of her early days. Also Spring/Summer 2027, her Erdem minidress featured a powder-blue high-neck bodice with cutouts and crystal embellishments, and a red satin skirt with a big bow and a raw hem. And of course, it wouldn’t be a country performance without cowboy boots, so here, the showgirl wore a light-blue pair, made custom by Sonora.

Later, Swift traded the bright hues for dark glamour, taking the stage in a midnight-blue Taller Marmo Fall 2026 dress to present the Willie Nelson Lifetime Achievement Award to musical duo Brooks & Dunn. The Disco Dragon Dress, as it’s called, was defined by oversize cape-like sleeves, with fringe and sequins galore. Stylist Joseph Cassell added jewels from Mindi Mond to bring everything together.

Swift last performed at the CMAs in 2013, when she received the prestigious Pinnacle Award and sang an acoustic version of “Red,” the title track off of her 2012 album. The following year, she would go on to release 1989, her official foray into the world of pop music, and would only return to Nashville’s award ceremony once more—in 2016, to present the Entertainer of the Year award to Garth Brooks.

A decade later, she returned, and the homecoming was as emotional as expected.

#6993. What Watch Will Robert Pattinson Wear as James Bond? It's Complicated.

Timepieces are almost as much a part of 007’s wardrobe as his dinner jackets.

Perhaps you've heard? There's a new James Bond in town!

Robert Pattinson has been announced as the next 007, and while most of the speculation has understandably focused on how the former vampire/current Batman might approach Britain's foremost secret agent, watch aficionados are asking another question: What will he wear on his wrist?

Watches, after all, are almost as much a part of Bond's wardrobe as his dinner jackets, Aston Martins, and vodka martinis. Sean Connery famously wore a Rolex Submariner, Roger Moore experimented with Seiko's digital watches, and Omega has been 007's watch of choice since Pierce Brosnan strapped on a trusty Seamaster in GoldenEye in 1995. That brand remains, of course, a paid partner of the franchise.

Yesterday's reveal showed no watch on the newly anointed 007's wrist, and Pattinson's own taste in watches is rather different from the rugged, sporty timepieces traditionally associated with Bond.

In fact, he doesn't seem particularly keen on big watches at all.

"Whenever a watch gets too thick and it keeps your cuff up, I don't really understand the appeal," he told one interviewer as recently September. "A lot of guys really like that, but I don't think I can really pull that off. I need to only have a half reveal."

And when asked what he looks for in a watch, he was even more specific.

"I definitely like really thin watches. That's the main thing. In general, I would probably prefer a leather strap."

Which is quite a contrast with the substantial divers we've become accustomed to seeing on Bond's wrist.

The fact is, until recently, Pattinson wasn't much of a watch enthusiast at all. That changed at this year's Oscars, when he wore a then-unreleased rose-gold Jaeger-LeCoultre Master Control Chronomètre Perpetual Calendar. Other actors apparently began asking him about it at the afterparty, and something clicked.

He subsequently became an ambassador for Jaeger-LeCoultre, although his relationship with the brand goes back much further. As a teenager, he borrowed his mother's Reverso to impress his schoolmates. More recently, he's revealed that Bruce Wayne will wear a Reverso in the next Batman film.

On screen, Pattinson's watch choices have been interesting. He wore a Chanel J12 in David Cronenberg's Cosmopolis, while Christopher Nolan's Tenet, in which he played a mysterious secret agent, featured a Bond-adjacent Omega De Ville.

Meanwhile, the announcement has prompted some entertaining activity on Instagram. Within hours of Pattinson being named Bond, Jaeger-LeCoultre posted pictures of its new ambassador wearing the Master Control Chronomètre Perpetual Calendar. Omega, meanwhile, posted an image of its James Bond Seamaster. And today, Jaeger-LeCoultre has been back with more Pattinson pictures.

It's a little social-media tit-for-tat, although there's no reason the two relationships can't coexist. For now, at least, there's a straightforward distinction: Pattinson can wear whatever he likes. Bond has his own watch.

#6990. All of the Easter Eggs You Might Have Missed From Taylor Swift’s CMA Performance

And why fans are convinced a re-recorded debut album is on its way

Last night, Taylor Swift made her grand return to the Country Music Awards. Taking the award show’s stage for the first time since 2013, the singer-songwriter performed a mashup of her Single of the Year-nominated Toy Story 5 track, “I Knew It, I Knew You,” and one of her OG country classics: “Our Song.” Released in 2006 as the third single off her debut, self-titled album, the song choice was an emotional tribute to her Nashville origins—reminding the crowd that before she was touring the world as a global pop star, she was writing lyrics in math class and knocking on the doors of Music Row to try to get a record deal.

But the inclusion of “Our Song” wasn’t the only nod to her roots. Throughout the performance, which followed Swift from a backstage dressing room through the Bridgestone Arena hallways and eventually to the stage, the Easter egg professional sprinkled in many references to her early days. And the Swifties are convinced the hints might mean a re-recorded version of her debut album is on its way. (When announcing she bought her masters in May 2025, Swift revealed that she had already recorded an entire “Taylor’s Version” of her first record—though she has been quiet about it ever since.)

Ahead, we break down all the Easter eggs you might have missed from Swift’s CMAs performance.

When the performance kicked off, we saw Swift getting ready in a dressing room backstage. At first glance, the whole place was covered in dresses—with several gowns draped on the plush couch and even more hung on a clothing rack. But once we looked closely, we realized that each of the pieces came from the star’s archive. There was the BCBG halter-neck dress worn to the 2006 Academy of Country Music Awards (ACMs), the sea foam-green maxi from the “Teardrops on My Guitar” music video, and, most notably, the champagne beaded Sandi Spika gown worn to the artist’s first CMAs. It was here that Swift was awarded with the 2007 Horizon Award at the age of 17, definitively declaring it to be the “highlight of [her] senior year.”

From the dressing room, Swift walked through the arena hallways, interacting with a handful of other artists (see here)—from Eric Church to Rascal Flatts to Sugarland to Keith Urban. Every person she encountered she had either opened for, collaborated with, or had a connection to from her early days. One exception was Lainey Wilson—making people guess that the country musician might be featured on the (unconfirmed) forthcoming re-record.

#6989. Brad Pitt Wants to Play a James Bond Villain

The announcement finally arrived this week: Robert Pattinson is James Bond. But before we could even come up for air about Amazon MGM and director Denis Villeneuve’s decision to have arguably the busiest movie star in the business today follow Daniel Craig as 007, the Internet had already moved on. Fans started asking more questions. Who will costar as the next Bond girl? Who’s performing the song? And, most importantly, who would play opposite Pattinson as the villain?

Well, how about Brad Pitt? The F1 star likely didn’t expect a James Bond question when he stepped on the red carpet for the premiere of his latest film, The Further Mis-Adventures of Cliff Booth. The spinoff sequel of Quentin Tarantino’s Once Upon a Time... in Hollywood sees Pitt return as stuntman Cliff Booth under the direction of David Fincher, which couldn’t be further from the life of a British MI6 hitman. Still, Pitt was excited to hear for the first time that Pattinson landed the role when a reporter for ExtraTV shared the news. “I love this idea,” Pitt said. “He’s the real deal.”

Would he ever think about playing the villain? Pitt winked at the camera and said, “Robert? Denis? You know what, I’d like to do that. My man Javier [Bardem] did it so well [in Skyfall] It’s tall shoes, but I’d take that on.”

Later down the red carpet, Pitt answered the question again when Complex asked if he had any advice for Pattinson. “Sign me for the villain,” he said, coming around to the idea even more. “Get Denis Villeneuve on the flip phone. Let’s go!”

Elsewhere, Pattinson’s fellow Dune: Part Three costar Florence Pugh is already pitching herself as a potential villain and/or Bond girl. When Villeneuve presented her with an honor at Elle's Women in Hollywood event on Monday, Pugh congratulated the director on finding the next James Bond. “Nice catch,” she said. “Any villains the size of a five-foot-four-inch female?" I’m sure this is just the beginning.

I’ll admit, Pitt wasn’t my first choice for the next Bond villain. If anything, Pattinson was. But I love how enthusiastic he is about the possibility. Now, I kind of feel like I need to see it.

#6988. A Full Timeline of Ella Langley and Tucker Wetmore’s Rumored Romance, Because It’s Very Confusing

If you’re a country music fan (or tbh, even if you’re not), then you’re probably well aware that the love lives of country music’s rising stars are quite entangled…and have been for a minute now. The latest (reported) update? Ella Langley and Tucker Wetmore are rumored to be dating, but we really can’t talk about their maybe-relationship without discussing Tucker’s ex Bryana Ferringer...or Ella’s rumored ex Riley Green (who went on to maybe date Bryana).

Ella and Tucker may have sparked dating rumors late last year, but it wasn’t without some drama on the way. It’s a pretty confusing ride, so buckle up and let’s get into it. Starting with…

After months of dating rumors following the success of Ella and Riley’s song “You Look Like You Love Me,” Ella finally shuts down Riley dating rumors. “We’re not dating,” she tells Taste of Country, adding, “We’re just good friends.”

Riley also shuts down dating speculation during an appearance on The Viall Files podcast. “Ella’s probably too smart to date me anyway,” he jokes, later adding, “When we first started having the success we did with that song, I kinda told Ella, I was like, ‘There’s gonna be a lot of things said, but I mean, you don’t really have to play into it or not play into it.’”

Tucker posts a TikTok on August 20 with the caption “@ my ex,” sparking breakup rumors with his model girlfriend Bryana (who, btw, appeared in his music video for “Wind Up Missin’ You”).

Soon after, Bryana is spotted with Riley at his Red Rocks tour stop.

Ella and Tucker both attend the 2025 BMI Country Awards on November 18, though it’s unclear whether this is the first time they ever meet (or if they even interacted, despite standing right next to each other in this group photo):

A day later, after several reported sightings around Nashville, Riley brings Bryana as his date to the CMA Awards on November 19.

However! Riley later refers to Bryana as merely a “friend,” telling Backstage Country, “It’s gonna be tough to tiptoe out of having a date to an award show. Yeah, I mean, you go to award shows all the time, and I’m always by myself, or I’ve got, like, a security guy, or a buddy with me, so I took a friend with me to the award show.”

Around the same time, Ella sparks dating rumors with Farmer Wants a Wife season 3 star John Sansone (whose cousin, music exec Sophia Sansone, previously dated Riley). Rumors begin after John includes a picture of Ella’s dog, Crue, in a November 26 Instagram photo dump...and then, on the same day, Ella includes a video of herself shooting shotguns with John in a photo dump of her own.

John later confirms to Taste of Country in April 2026 that he and Ella “dated for a couple of months” after he slid into her DMs but are no longer together.

Oh, and side note: John’s ex from Farmer Wants a Wife, Samantha Riehl, posted a TikTok with Tucker back in April 2025 with the caption, “Blondes are the worst.”

And then right after, John posted a TikTok with Bryana at Stagecoach (which has since been deleted).

Pretty sure nothing ever happened between Samantha and Tucker (or between Bryana and John), but still, it feels worth mentioning!!

On December 5, Tucker releases “Proving Me Right,” which fans are preeeetty convinced is about his ex, Bryana.

A few weeks later, Ella and Tucker are photographed together in the British Virgin Islands at the Soggy Dollar Bar...but considering the photo also includes other country artists, rumors circulate that the trip was possibly a writing retreat.

Tucker and Ella reunite to sing a live acoustic duet of Tucker’s “Sunburn” at the Heart Strings for Hope charity event on March 10.

And while Tucker didn’t write “Sunburn” himself, a few of the lyrics kiiiinda seem to allude to his December getaway with Ella.

Tucker then appears on the March 17 episode of Kristin Cavallari’s Let’s Be Honest podcast, where he claims that he has “never been broken up with” and has always been the one to “call it quits.” When Kristin asks if he has “sworn off blondes,” Tucker says, “Yep. I have. For the foreseeable future. That’s right.”

And then, a few days after that on March 21, Tucker posts an Instagram photo of himself holding a fish, and Ella slides into his comments with, “Cute fish.” Flirty!

Tucker discusses his hit “Brunette” with Audacy, saying, “My mom’s always said, ‘You're going to mess around with blondes for a little while but I truly believe you're going to settle down with the brunette,’ and I was like, ‘Yeah, 100 percent.’”

Not long after, during an appearance on Taste of Country Nights, Tucker notes, “Pretty much every girlfriend I have ever had was blonde, and I think it’s time to change up a couple of things.”

On September 23 (just a day after Ella breaks Mariah Carey’s record for longest-running Billboard Hot 100 hit with “Choosin’ Texas”), TMZ breaks the news that Tucker and Ella are (reportedly) dating.

The outlet obtains photos of the two out with friends in Nashville on the night of September 22 following the Nashville Songwriter Awards, with sources telling TMZ that the two met “through the music industry and mutual friends.”

Multiple sources also confirm the rumored romance to People so…it seems legit to me!

While making an appearance at WAVE in Wichita, Kansas, a fan straight-up asks Tucker whether he and Ella “are a thing.”

Tucker’s two-word response? “Next question.” Okay!!

Ella and Tucker seemingly take another trip to the Virgin Islands…at least, according to a sneaky fan who spotted them entering a restaurant together.

Soon after, they attend the CMAs, where they walk the carpet separately but still can’t dodge questions about their relationship status. When asked by People if there is a certain brunette he’s rooting for, he jokingly answers, “Zach Top or Stephen Wilson Jr.” Mmmkay!

Ella is also caught grinning during Tucker’s performance, which...isn’t confirmation of anything but certainly telling.

#6986. Wait, Did Hailey Bieber Buy CBK’s Jacket on the RealReal?

Well, no, but there’s a lot to unpack here

Yesterday, Hailey Bieber took her obsession with ’90s minimalism one step further with a reference to the ultimate ‘90s minimalist: Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy. For a talk at the 2026 DealBook Summit here in New York, she and her stylist Dani Michelle pulled a Yohji Yamamoto look from Spring 1998, the one with a black, high-necked jacket with a line of delicate buttons paired with a white linen column skirt so famously worn by CBK in June 1998. It did what it was supposed to do: Stop us in our scrolling. But it jogged something else in the collective consciousness of fashion lovers everywhere. Hadn’t this jacket just made the rounds on our feeds?

A few weeks ago, savvy shoppers spotted the look for sale on the RealReal. Bessette-Kennedy’s reverence for Yohji Yamamoto is one that fashion’s acolytes tend to share, so it didn’t take long for the listing to go viral. The description identified its starry provenance: “From the Spring/Summer 1998 Collection; as worn by Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy in 1998 and worn by Winona Ryder for the season 5 premiere of 'Stranger Things' in 2025.”

TRR listed the jacket for $7,025. It sat on the site long enough to make the Instagram rounds, but someone snatched it up before the site had time to mark it down. The jacket sold for full price on November 6, less than two weeks before the DealBook Summit.

Could it be that Bieber scrolls for vintage Yohji on the RealReal like the rest of us? Does she have saved searches? Is she watching her obsessions with the eyes of a hawk? (Or has she outsourced this job to Michelle or someone on her team?) Bazaar reached out to the RealReal, and a spokesperson revealed that while all customer data is private, they know that Bieber’s look did not come from them. Well, it was fun to imagine. But just the possibility that this could have happened is enough to make you think.

Michelle’s post of the look does not reveal her source, though days later, she confirmed the source to be Tab Vintage. The vintage resale platform Shy mentioned in comment sections that it recently sold the same piece. Another one of the jackets, also without a proven link to CBK’s personal wardrobe, sold via the Fashion Auctioneer in March for $12,500. That’s the thing with ready-to-wear: It’s not one-of-a-kind. Bessette-Kennedy bought her jacket off the rack, which means there are multiple versions floating around out there.

If you scroll down the jacket’s sold page on the RealReal to the “Related Items” section, you’ll find multiple listings of the Cruise 2005 Chanel jacket Andy Sachs famously wore in The Devil Wears Prada, priced at roughly ten grand. “Similar style seen on Anne Hathaway in 'The Devil Wears Prada,'” the description reads. “We are seeing more and more archival pieces with incredible cultural provenance come in on consignment,” says Kristen Naiman, chief brand officer at the RealReal. “And we love helping these shards of fashion history resurface in pop culture.”

It’s become common practice for major stylists to mine specialty vintage stores for these megawatt, nostalgia-driven red carpet references. Just look at Law Roach’s Instagram account, where he documents pulling 1950s Balenciaga for Kim Kardashian from Lily et Cie, a source that specializes in museum-quality vintage. But as this thought experiment exemplifies, these transactions are becoming more normalized.

Naiman continues: “Whether you know Carolyn Bessette-Kennedy or not, there is now an entire new generation coveting this Yohji Yamamoto look on Hailey Bieber, and it has a renewed desirability because of that. And that’s the fun of being a fashion hobbyist and TRR enthusiast. You not only love the piece, you know the love story behind the piece. It’s a way to participate in fashion history. You can spend years coveting a jacket that has left an indelible mark on culture and then, one day, find it in your size.”

Which brings us back to that Yohji jacket that sold on November 6. We know Bieber wasn’t the one who purchased it, but some lucky person did, and now they own a piece of fashion history. Wonder how many other people have the same one.

Per our pros, "it combines retinol with a hoard of lightweight hydrators—including glycerin, hyaluronic acid, and vitamin E—to help counteract the sensitizing effect of retinol without feeling too heavy."

Call me basic, but I do have an unhealthy attachment to my Owala water bottle—its sip-or-chug spout design is unmatched. You can save 20% on select color combinations, like this pastel blue and green pick.

Not only does this compact coffee maker come in cute colors like this petal pink hue, but it can whip up espresso shots quicker and more easily than your standard espresso machine. #6979. The AI Police Are Going After Black Writers

The white powers that be have always doubted our intellect. Now they have a new tool.

Picture a waifish enslaved teen—a girl named Phillis, after the slave ship that Middle Passaged her from present-day Senegal and Gambia—trembling before a room of incredulous, austere white men, the most esteemed thinkers and politicians of colonial Massachusetts. The reason: The gifted girl wrote a book manuscript, and the family that owned her journeyed all the way to London to find a publisher for it. That prospective British publisher, a bloke named Archibald Bell, promised to publish the manuscript if the Wheatleys could prove Phillis had written it herself—an attestation tantamount, of course, to whether an enslaved African soon-to-be American possessed the intellect requisite to producing exceptional creative work.

Present at the trial, which has been described by historian Henry Louis Gates Jr. as the nascent scene of African American letters, was Thomas Hutchinson, the governor of the Province of the Massachusetts Bay; Andrew Oliver, the province’s lieutenant governor; and Mr. John Hancock himself, the future first governor of the Commonwealth of Massachusetts. Per Gates, almost all the men forgathered to pass judgment on Phillis Wheatley were both Harvard graduates and slaveholders. No record exists of what transpired, but oh how brilliant Phillis must’ve been as she convinced those 18 eminent Bostonians that she’d indeed written the poems. The confirmation was printed in the front matter of Phillis’s poetry collection—the first book published by an African soon-to-be American—Poems on Various Subjects, Religious and Moral (1773):

We whose Names are under-written, do assure the World, that the Poems specified in the following Page, were . . . written by Phillis, a young Negro Girl, who was but a few Years since, brought an uncultivated Barbarian from Africa, and has ever since been, and now is, under the Disadvantage of serving as a Slave in a Family in this Town. She has been examined by some of the best Judges, and is thought qualified to write them.

As my great-grandmother Edith Larkin Jackson, a woman not two generations removed from chattel slavery, used to say, “There’s nothing new under the sun.” Mama Eddie was a schoolteacher, and I imagine she’d be astounded by artificial intelligence. However, she’d have no trouble whatsoever fathoming a world that called into question the creativity or intellect of her people.

Here we are in this brave new world of AI, an age hurtling headlong toward the peril of superintelligence. Here we are in the 21st century, a time when the ivory-tower panjandrums are engaged in a sinister pattern, if not conspiratorial scheme, of incriminating Black and brown writers with even the suggestion that their work is AI assisted.

For example: In March, Hachette Book Group rendered kaput the U. S. publication of Mia Ballard’s Shy Girl over claims of AI usage.

When Trinidadian writer Jamir Nazir’s story “The Serpent in the Grove” was named the regional winner of the Commonwealth Prize in May, Ethan Mollick, a Wharton Business School professor who serves as the codirector of Wharton Generative AI Labs, ran it through the AI-detection software Pangram, touted as the industry standard, and reported that the platform flagged it at 100 percent. Once Mollick shared his findings on social media, several others joined the chorus bemoaning Nazir’s work as a product of AI.

In July, academics studying AI accused H. M. Wolfe of having used a large language model to write her best seller Daggermouth (though its publisher Simon & Schuster has, thus far, stood by the book). Later that month, writer Jerry Falade had a $2 million book deal with Macmillan collapse after his agents withdrew the submission of his debut crime novel citing AI concerns.

In September, Haitian Canadian writer Thélyson Orélien’s novel C’était Ça ou Mourir (It Was Either That or Die) was proclaimed a standout of the French literary season, won France’s Prix du Roman FNAC, and was long-listed for the Goncourt, the country’s most prestigious literary prize. But then an anonymous X account (later revealed to be owned by a conservative columnist) posted that Pangram had identified all 34 chapters of the book as having been written with AI. That claim spurred accusations that Orélien plagiarized earlier work, leading the Goncourt to nix the book from its long list.

In October, The Atlantic accused writer Simbarashe Kundizeza of using AI to write “The Blue Fighter,” which won the Caine Prize, Africa’s most prestigious short-story accolade.

Germane: While Nazir is Trinidadian, Ballard, Wolfe, Falade, Orélien, and Kundizeza are all Black.

Although all those writers have denied claims of using AI to produce their work, I’m not here to dispute whether they did or to what degree they used it, nor the epistemological paradox of using AI to determine whether a writer used AI to write a story. Of equal importance is that, if we ain’t reached the point where all published writing is scanned for possible use of an LLM, what’s the logic informing which writers are investigated for AI use and which aren’t, as well as the attendant issue of which accused writers suffer the most severe fallout, including the scarlet letter of damaged credibility?

The plight of Black and brown writers in the age of AI must be judged in light of what’s being described as an era of postliteracy. Of the specter of AI sloptimism becoming so ubiquitous it overruns human output. Of the cataclysmic doom of superintelligence approximating the creative work of a human brain to such a degree that the difference will be negligible to the undiscerning masses.

The plight of Black and brown writers in the age of AI must be apprehended against the beef between the publishing industry writ large and AI. Against 10,000 authors having copublished an “empty” book in protest of their work being pirated to train its models. Against America’s Big Five publishers suing Meta for copyright infringement. Against the recent unsealed documents that reveal OpenAI executives knew they were training their AI on copyrighted material and, furthermore, that AI-authored books were apt to displace in the market ones authored by humans. “There will be a point where a bunch of artists express worry about what we’re doing here, and we’ll likely ignore their concerns and release anyway,” OpenAI policy director Jack Clark wrote in an internal memo back in 2020. The predicament of Black and brown writers should be weighed against the long and calculated history of portraying them as lesser intellects.

Casting doubt on the possibility of Black literary brilliance is in the spirit of a hubristic Saul Bellow asking, “Who is the Tolstoy of the Zulus?” Of 45/47 portraying beaucoup Black people as “not that bright.” Of attacking DEI to paint us as unworthy or incapable, less we are sanctioned by people in power. The same insidious forces—linked to right-wing Cambridge professor James Orr and conservative billionaire Peter Thiel—stoked the allegations that ended in Cambridge professor Jason Arday dying by suicide. (Arday has been posthumously cleared of plagiarism.)

In stark contrast, best-selling novelist and screenwriter Anthony Horowitz has admitted using AI for his work. Best-selling biographer Katie Prescott extolled AI as invaluable in writing her book on Mike Lynch, the late British tech entrepreneur and AI pioneer. And while she’s been called among other things a “grifter” and a “villain,” South African writer Coral Hart has published more than 370 books using artificial intelligence since 2025 and defended her use of LLMs despite criticism and threats. While she later clarified she used it only for research, Nobel laureate Olga Tokarczuk caused a ruckus revealing she queried AI for writing help. Other than Santiago Schnell, a white Latino, resigning as the provost of Dartmouth for using AI in his published articles and The New York Times cutting ties with Alex Preston for an AI-assisted book review, beyond vocal to tepid criticism, white writers aren’t suffering material consequences on par with those of their Black peers.

Poor Phillis Wheatley never published a second book. Despite advertising six times in the newspaper to find a publisher. Despite announcing she’d dedicate it to Benjamin Franklin. Despite General George Washington extolling her as “favored by the muses.” In fact, in 1784, Phillis died at 30 years young, destitute, having placed the last advertisement for her second manuscript just a few months prior.

Because Big Tech is big-time thriving and race is evermore an oppressive tool, critics are bound to keep casting the work of Black writers as AI assisted, in effect electing themselves nouveau versions of those privileged 18th-century Bostonians. Best believe their indictments are a tacit way to portray the work of Black people as unworthy of patronage, acclaim, awards—aka inferior. But be forewarned: If Big Tech succeeds at AI turning the creative work of human brains passé if not obsolete, it’s near foregone that most writers will lose their ability to earn a living. Indeed, the sad-ass upshot is that if AI prevails, all writers—race be damned—will be fated a Phillis.

#6972. 8 Popular Home Items From 20 Years Ago That Are Making a Surprising Comeback

They may be a blast from the past, but they’re about to be very now.

Trends come and go, but they almost always come back again. If there’s one thing we know about interior design, it’s that what feels old and outdated manages to become fresh and cool again in a few years’ time. Skeptics can easily be proved wrong with the resurgence of sunken living rooms, glass block walls, and even cushy sofas plucked right out of a Nora Ephron movie. And, as younger generations continue to be intrigued with the 1990s and early 2000s, they are turning to the era’s interiors for inspiration.

Well, sort of, as Maggie Vollrath of Provision Studios points out. “When we look back at certain interiors from the 2000s, there was such a commitment to achieving a particular look that every element in the room was chosen to reinforce it,” the Georgia designer says. “A home should feel like a collection of things you genuinely love, not as though you’ve walked onto a movie set designed to represent a particular style.”

When you cherry-pick trends and design details from the past, you’re able to create a home that honors the past without appearing stuck in the past. “The goal isn’t to make a home look like it belongs to another decade,” Vollrath says. “You want to bring back the beauty and character of those decorative details while creating something that feels authentic to both the house and its owners.”

Below, designers share trends from the past that the next generation of design lovers adore.

For older generations, a swivel chair might conjure memories of sitting in your family’s den, but designers promise these movin’, groovin’ seats have been revived for the 21st century. “Swivel barrel chairs defined early-2000s transitional interiors, usually in a bright flat polyester-blend upholstery,” explains Hallie Malitsky, principal designer of Hallie Rose Design in Brooklyn. “The version coming back today is a different experience entirely.”

Today’s iterations, she insists, are subtler. Cheap polyester has yielded to rich, tactile fabrics like wool, linen, and velvet. For a timely twist, she also encourages trading the fire engine red and butter yellow popular in the 2000s for burgundy and ochre. “Surround it with natural materials like wood, linen, and stone, and [your chair] will stop being the loudest thing in the room,” she recommends.

When Malitsky thinks of early aughts design, she immediately envisions the gigantic walnut media center that plagued many of her friends’ family rooms. “Dark wood cabinetry is a notorious culprit of bad 2000s design, but the wood wasn’t the issue,” she says. “It was the fact that there was so much of it.”

Now that dark wood tones are making a comeback, Malitsky says to use them sparingly. Two to three walnut or ebony pieces per room will make enough of a statement. And, no: Unlike the living rooms of yore, your furniture doesn’t have to match.

“What I love about their return is that we’re incorporating these pieces in a much more collected way,” shares Vollrath. “Rather than purchasing an entire matching furniture collection, we’re mixing periods and bringing in individual pieces that have character and history.”

The early aughts had lots of elaborate cabinetry, replete with corbels, substantial cornices, and beautifully detailed range hoods. As everyone—yes, even younger generations—continue to favor more traditional interiors, we’re seeing a renewed interest in ornamented cabinetry.

“There’s something really special about a kitchen that feels as though it could have been there for generations, or even reminds you of your grandmother’s kitchen,” Vollrath says. “Those decorative flourishes bring a sense of history, craftsmanship, and permanence that can be difficult to achieve with a more streamlined approach.”

But why stop there when you can add character to other rooms of the house?

“We’re seeing a broader interest in architectural detailing throughout the home, from wainscoting and wall paneling to ceiling beams and substantial moldings,” she adds. “Homeowners are becoming more interested in understanding these elements, considering them during renovations, and making them a priority in new construction.”

If the rise of analog interiors proves anything, it’s that even the best chatbot can’t beat excellent design. Instead of having a home covered in screens and smart systems, many are favoring more primitive setups that harken back to dial-up and CD-ROMs. For example, Fargo-based designer Lindsey Christie has been noticing (and loving) the return of landlines. “There's something wonderfully nostalgic about a beautiful telephone sitting on a bedside table or tucked into a thoughtfully designed corner of a home,” she says.

Of course, there’s plenty of fun to be had in a “dumb home.” “We have had several adult clients wanting to incorporate their hobby of Legos into their design aesthetic,” reveals Allison Smith of Gem + Clay Interiors in St. Augustine, Florida. “I definitely think this speaks to a desire to return to the analog, and that [old-school] element.”

“We always joke about the Tuscan kitchens from 2005, but they had some good things going on,” says Drew Michael Scott of Lone Fox. If you strip away the “bottles of preserved peppers and gaudy oil-rubbed bronze faucets,” the style was actually rocking a great color palette. Shades like brown, ochre, terracotta, and deep red bring a little bit of the great outdoors inside, plus they can pair nicely with today’s cozy interiors. But let’s keep the overly Italian influences in the past, shall we?

Today’s emerging design lovers are extra—just ask Scott, who has been noticing a considerable uptick in passementerie. “These intricate details were everywhere in the mid-2000s, before the world decided everything should be sleek and gray,” he says. “They’re becoming popular again, and I think they really evoke the attention to detail we saw at the turn of the century.”

Though yesteryear’s tassels, beading, and decorative trim are undeniably fun, using them requires a little bit of strategy. Since there’s a fine line between charming and kitschy, balance these louder, upholstered moments with pared-back wooden pieces.

Goodbye, panel-ready refrigerators and dishwashers: Katie Kiser says stainless steel appliances are so back. According to the Tennessee-based designer, the resurgence is a break from the picture-perfect kitchens older generations have pined over. “Gen-Z values practicality over perfection,” she explains. “I think it will show up in their interiors.” And why stop at appliances when you can bring that lived-in feel to the rest of the space? “They don’t hate clutter the way millennials do,” she adds. “We’ll see details like cookbooks in the kitchens and hall trees with tennis rackets on them.”

Though Scott hasn’t seen the Venetian Gold granite that ruled Y2K just yet, he’s noticing many homeowners are willing to get creative with their countertops.

“We've moved beyond using only Carrara marble in kitchens,” he explains.

Ditto for quartz, which some designers believe is on the outs. Instead, Scott says travertine is huge again and granite is poised to make the comeback. If you want to keep these materials from appearing dated, he encourages you to reach for stones with bolding veining and inclusions.

#6968. 36 Thoughtful Kitchen Gifts for Anyone Who Loves to Cook, Host, or Sip in Style

Every item on this page was hand-picked by a House Beautiful editor. We may earn commission on some of the items you choose to buy.

Anyone can buy a candle. These gifts are way more personal.

The best kind of gift shows the person you care about that you know them. That you’ve considered their hobbies. After all, anyone can go out and buy a scented candle, but you are thinking about how they like to spend their free time and what makes them happy. So if you know a dinner party host, mixologist, home chef, or someone who loves a stylish kitchen, then this is the gift guide for you.

Below, dozens of smart and thoughtful kitchen gifts await you. The busy home cook will love an Instant Pot or multipurpose air fryer. The coffee or tea obsessive will love a matcha station or coffee grinder. Think outside the kitchen and into the dining room, too: an elegant yet budget-friendly canister set or berry basket could be just what they need to complete their tablescape.

The best thing about so many of our picks is that there’s a massive price range, so if you see something affordable your friend or family member may like, you don’t need to wait for a special occasion to get it for them. On the other hand, if the same person is celebrating a birthday or a holiday, feel free to splurge. We have everything any at-home cook would love and use.

No kitchen is complete without a good bottle opener, so why not opt for one that’s extra chic? Trade your gas station souvenir opener for this colorful beaded piece, inspired by the classic abacus tool. You can even rearrange the beads to suit your own aesthetic.

Any serious coffee drinker or amateur barista will know the importance of a good coffee bean grinder. There’s nothing quite like being able to control the coarseness of your own coffee for your morning brew. You can choose between 60 grind sizes, whether you’re using a moka pot or prepping cold brew.

If your date night dinner isn’t complete without a round of oysters, then this oyster plate needs to be added to your collection. The ridged porcelain edge looks like a more elegant version of a paper plate. And the indents in the dish are perfect for your East Coast dozen. It’s even dishwasher-safe.

This is the ideal martini glass for your most stylish friend. The color-blocked stems are thin enough for a swirl, and the set of two glasses comes in alternating colors. It even comes with stainless steel swizzle sticks, primed for stuffed olives.

Gift a set of serving utensils that’s a major upgrade from the typical silicone and plastic. The lustrous finish and curved handles are just too beautiful. It’s carved from sustainably sourced horn and natural shell, and it’ll make your salad bowl look like the star of the show.

Rocco’s full-size fridges have gone viral for every sophisticated host. But the latest launch is way more budget-friendly. This mini mini fridge is sized for desktops and nightstands, so whether you need a nighttime face mask or a midday Diet Coke break, it’s stored in style.

Everyone knows at least one matcha drinker who is serious about their habit. This is the station they’ll reach for every morning. The bowl, bamboo whisk, whisk stand, and sifter all ensure a clump-free, perfectly aerated brew. And it’s all gathered on a ceramic, non-slip tea tray.

Know a tinned fish lover? Fishwife has made sardines sexy again, and this advent calendar provides 12 days of fun surprises for every charcuterie board assembler. It even comes with a few non-edible pieces like a fish knife, key chain, and moisturizer.

Associate Shopping Editor Janae McKenzie has been using the electric kettle from Drew Barrymore’s line for years. It comes in a variety of pastel colors that brighten up your countertop and make short work of everything from loose-leaf tea to soup.

Any home chef serious about their spices needs a mortar and pestle that can keep up. It’s made from pure bronze, with an ergonomic handle that makes grinding peppercorns, cardamom, ginger, and garlic easy. It’s handmade by artisans in Moradabad, known as “The Brass City of India.”

These glasses look like they should be kept safe in a china cabinet, but they’re durable enough for everyday life. The shape of the glass was designed in collaboration with WhistlePig distillers to enhance the whiskey-drinking experience. The pontil mark on the bottom of each glass celebrates “perfect imperfection”—a mark leftover by Simon Pearce glassblowers.

A new paper towel holder is an upgrade you won’t think to do on your own, which makes it the perfect kitchen gift. It’s likely one of the most-visited spots in your kitchen. The painted beech wood and rubber feet keep it both stylish and secure.

Things get messy in the kitchen, and no one will say no to a gift of fresh dish towels. These are woven from organic cotton and stitched by a woman-owned textile company in Mumbai. They’re embroidered with the icons of Indian cooking: a fresh coconut, a tomato, and curry leaves.

Ash wood and granite stone are a match made in heaven to get the most of your ground pepper. McKenzie uses this with gourmet peppercorns, and with the easily refilled vessel and cut stone, it’s a tactile experience that elevates the simplest meal. The stone smooths over time as oils are released by the peppercorns, effectively seasoning it over time.

This is perfect for the pitmaster in your life. Sure, they've probably got a meat thermometer from the grocery store, but this upgrade is so worth it, especially for those long, slow-cooked meats. This high-tech, long-range Bluetooth thermometer will save them the headache of constantly checking their grill, so they can check its status from the comfort of the couch.

This is the potluck host’s secret weapon. No need to mess with the hassle of foil pans, chafing dishes, and Sterno burners. Just one glass-top electric warming tray can keep your appetizers hot for the whole party. It even comes with cool-touch handles for easy portability.

This is the hand wash McKenzie leaves out on her kitchen sink when guests come over. “The Moroccan mint scent is refreshing, and the lather is moisturizing, not stripping,” McKenzie says. “When the dinner party gets chaotic, this is the easiest way for guests to refresh themselves in the kitchen. The glass bottle is an extra sleek touch.”

There's a reason Drew Barrymore's slow cooker sold out within days of launching. Now, more than a year later, the hype has gone down a bit, making snatching one up a lot easier. It comes in a few delicious colors, but we're partial to the white one, which Barrymore aptly named White Icing.

#6967. A Roundtable of Men in Their Nineties Taught Me the Secrets of Longevity

Every single thing I learned is something you can start doing right now.

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Alan Patricof, 92, is one of my idols. The legendary venture capitalist was an early investor in enterprises as varied as AOL, Apple, and New York magazine. More important to me, aside from his incredible career and philanthropic work, he is a man who believes in always moving forward into the future. He co-launched a fund called Primetime Partners when he was 85. At 88, he wrote his first book, No Red Lights: Reflections on Life, 50 Years in Venture Capital and Never Driving Alone. He was the oldest participant—and finisher—in the New York City Marathon at 88 and got remarried at 89 to the globally recognized art advisor Barbara Guggenheim.

So when Patricof—who has also become one of my friends—called me with a request to join in on a lunch he was hosting for 12 men in their 90s, I was intrigued. I have been reporting on longevity for several years now and lead a company called ROARforward focused on the subject. Patricof asked me to come and talk about what I had learned about longevity and the possibility of the 100-year life. I agreed, but I immediately thought of it differently—as a master class in wisdom and experience where I stood to learn at least as much from these men as they could learn from me.

When I saw the list of guests, it was a bit intimidating. It included a former CEO of a Fortune 100 company, a vice chairman emeritus at one of the largest banks on the planet, a major real estate developer, and a man who has his name on one of the most prestigious health systems in New York. And those are just the ones who agreed to be identified—some asked to remain anonymous.

You get the idea: This was not an everyday group of 90-somethings but rather a gathering of highly accomplished, successful men who continue to thrive in a variety of industries and disciplines. That only made me more interested, though I imagined it might take some work to translate their habits and the ways they were spending their time into things more accessible to everyday people.

Luckily, I would turn out to be wrong about that.

As the men assembled in a private room at Patroon, a popular Manhattan restaurant, I saw that many of them already knew each other, some from as far back as grade school. As Patricof went over ground rules, including no talk of politics (both parties were represented) and no talk of sex (though Viagra did find its way into the conversation), they moved around the room, shaking hands, asking about wives and kids, and explaining what was keeping them busy.

That was my first clue that the behaviors that helped these men stay robust into their tenth decade had little to do with wealth. Research has shown that cultivating deep, healthy relationships is one of the best things you can do to increase your chances of having a long, healthy life. These men were living examples of what that looks like, and it’s free. (Though not if you have your lunches at Patroon.)

These men took care of three major aspects of their lives: health, head, and heart. First, they were committed to a fitness regimen and healthy eating habits. Second, they were highly engaged and curious individuals who valued a strong sense of purpose. Third, each of them had deep relationships with loved ones, whether a spouse, family, or friends.

You may notice that these are all universal virtues. They’re also things that can give you a sense of fulfillment if you’re only in your 80s, or 70s, or even younger. They aren’t solely investments in the future. You do them now, and they benefit you now. Which is a good feeling and precisely what makes longevity worth it in the first place. As one of the men said, “If today is the last day of my life, I’ve had a helluva run.”

“At 90, I tried boxing at a gym near my home in Harlem,” said George Hirsch, 92, before adding, proudly, that he went a full round. Hirsch was the longtime chairman of the New York Roadrunners running club. While he is no longer out doing road races, he is on a treadmill four or five days a week in a walk-run regimen. He supplements that with rowing, the elliptical machine, weights, and, apparently, the occasional prizefight, in a 60-minute routine.

For a gathering men who were 90 or older, this was a remarkably healthy group. It’s true they all have had access to the best health care, but none had problems with issues that come for everyone, like cognitive decline, diabetes, and heart disease. That word routine seemed to be the key. Routine, regimen—take your pick. These men make a point of committing to physical activity rather than just dabbling in it. One told me, “I think the secret to my life physically is that I’ve worked with the same trainer three days a week for 29 years,” adding that “even when I travel, I take my iPad and work out with him wherever I am.”

In some cases, these routines would be the envy of any younger man! The men had all played some type of sports when they were younger and carried that behavior deep into their adult lives. Since movement and exercise are the top natural medicines for a long, healthy life, these guys were on it. Activities included walking, weight training, stationary bicycling, swimming, pickleball, tennis, and downhill skiing. Two of them have a regular tennis game, and one of the oldest in the group is still an avid golfer. Most of them go to a gym or have a personal trainer. While a few admitted that they were slower as they got older—a sign of remarkable maturity in a man, to admit that—they were all active.

They are also conscious of what they eat. They agreed that they consume less food than in the past and drink very little, if at all. As Hirsch said, “I never drank much, maybe a beer or glass of wine with a meal. But I don’t drink anymore—it just doesn’t mean that much to me.”

Their attention to eating habits stood out. One thing that especially interested them was GLP-1’s, because of their impact on diet but also other potential benefits. As I shared what I’ve learned about longevity, including the frontiers of research on the topic, they asked question after question about the emergence of GLP-1’s and other classes of drugs that may enhance individual longevity. That kind of constant, engaged questioning was a hallmark of the group.

Only one of the men at the lunch defined himself as “retired.” The others said they were, at most, “semiretired.” While they are not necessarily working full throttle in what was their main career, to a man they all have very busy days—and that busyness, I found, is driven by a voracious curiosity, which is one of the best ways to strengthen and maintain brain health.

Ken Langone is a great example. He came from a working-class family in Queens and is still in awe of his good fortune. He’s taken it to mean that, as long as he’s able, he should be doing everything he can not to squander what he has. “I won big, and I can’t tell you how grateful I am,” he said, adding, “We should be giving, not taking.”

He is in his office three days a week and works from home two days a week, engaged in a public board, three private boards, his family office, and other investments. His commitment to public health is incredibly important to him; he serves as chair emeritus of NYU Langone Health.

“At 91,” Langone says, “my biggest issue is boredom, sitting around with nothing to do.”

Jim Freund, also 91, was a high-powered attorney into his early 60s, did a stint as a mediator after that, and now has a robust life as a writer, photographer, and musician.

He’s written 20 fiction and nonfiction books and has produced 25 albums of music featuring him at the piano. For six years, he has circulated a monthly blog for more than 500 friends and colleagues that includes the details of various projects he’s working on—an essay or short fiction, a piece of music, a portfolio of photos.

One expression of this curiosity is that all the men want to keep current. They follow the news, are dedicated readers, and have a deep curiosity about emerging trends. They don’t just read print; they’re on their phones, Kindles, Audible—anywhere there’s action. And AI—they’re on ChatGPT like everyone else. Some even have digital assistants.

But being current doesn’t just mean trendspotting. It can also mean having hobbies and projects that force you to engage with the world as it is. All of these men had ambitious new endeavors in the works.

Hirsch now has a bimonthly podcast with the running legend Amby Burfoot called “Running: State of the Sport.” Dan Rose, 97, former president and chairman of Rose Associates, the company that built Pentagon City and One Financial Center in Boston, is writing a new book. Patricof and his wife are taking Yiddish lessons. At least one man reported that he was finally learning the piano.

For all of them, it is important that their activities, whatever they may be, connect to deeper meaning and purpose.

Larry Leeds, 97, the former CEO and chairman of the apparel company Manhattan Industries, now works at Buckingham Capital Management five days a week buying and selling securities. It allows him to keep up with trends and investments. But it’s far from the most important thing to which he’s dedicated his energy.

Leeds hopes the leading line in his obituary is the $27 million he raised to launch the Cary Leeds Center for Tennis and Learning in the South Bronx in 2015. Dedicated to athletic instruction and academic enrichment for underprivileged youth—health and head—the center is also an expression of the most important thing in Leeds’s life: his family. It is named after his son, who competed in six US Opens and five Wimbledon championships before dying at the age of 45.

Sitting there in Patroon, I heard stories of how these men had committed to keeping themselves healthy and how their curiosity led them to continue to engage with the world at a point in their lives when they could just as easily have settled into insular routine. But it was the lunch itself that testified to their commitment to their hearts. Ninety years in, they were still making a point of getting together, speaking, laughing, and growing closer to other people.

Upon arriving, as we sat and introduced ourselves, Patricof explained that he wanted to do this lunch not only to learn from one another at this stage of life but also to simply enjoy socializing with peers—like many women do on a regular basis. Women live five to six more years than men; maybe it is their commitment to deep relationships, he suggested, that is one of the key factors of their greater longevity.

Women came up in another way too: Everyone attributed their health and longevity to having a loving partner.

The majority of the men have had incredibly long marriages. Nine of the 12 had been married for more than 60 years. Two had been married for 70 years. Then there was Freund, who joked that he, too, has been married for 60 years—just not all to the same woman. (He is married to his second wife, and they are at 41 years and counting.)

They all also have children and grandchildren, whom they are in touch with more than a few times a week—something any of us who are parents can do.

Rose has a unique ritual. “We have a family video conference every Sunday night with family from all parts of the country. It’s life enhancing and a time to connect and give everyone a chance to share accomplishments and disappointments with each other,” he explained.

At this stage of their lives, many have lost longtime friends, but they all strive to make new friends, some young, some old. In fact, there’s already a plan in place to replicate the lunch so that a bunch of other 90-somethings can be invited into the conversation. Here’s the thing I noticed about this: What got these guys excited about another lunch wasn’t simply that they enjoyed each other’s company or having a laugh with peers about Viagra. They were also inspired by one other. That must count for something.

I walked away inspired, too, and in awe of how these men live. They have to walk a line that most of us don’t yet, and maybe never will. Part of being alive—especially for this group—is thinking about the future, trying to figure out what’s next, planning another pursuit, but now they all admit that their horizon of looking out, say, 25 years is no longer a reality.

In the face of that tension, they’ve found a way to be optimistic about their future while at the same time savoring the present. None complained about getting older. None talked about their aches and pains. Yes, they faced growing limitations, but they all seemed to take them in stride.

“I do my best to love my life and try to have the same intensity and diligence that I had in my younger years,” said Leeds.

Langone said, “When I can no longer take care of myself and I’m totally dependent on someone else, then that’s the time to go,” adding that “God has his plan.”

And Patricof has his. He’s already publicly stated that he plans to live to 114, which would be 22 more years. I pointed out that the world’s oldest verified living person, Ethel Caterham in the UK, just turned 117, and I challenged Alan to break that record. He gladly told me that he accepts the challenge, and I don’t doubt he’ll make a real run at it. I have to admit I have an ulterior motive: I look forward to my lunch with centenarians. And supercentenarians.

#6962. The Latest PlayStation Is 6 Years Old and Twice as Expensive as When It Debuted, What’s That About?

Every product was carefully curated by an Esquire editor. We may earn a commission from these links. Here’s how we test products and why you should trust us.

Thanks to AI and the RAMpocalypse, it’ll cost you to play the new ‘GTA VI’.

Grand Theft Auto VI is here, and it’s going to shock people heading into the holidays. No, we’re not moralizing about whatever violence, sex, or illegal activity is in the game. We’re talking about sticker shock.

Do you have any idea how much a new entry-level PlayStation 5 costs now? It costs $600, yet it was only $400 when it debuted in 2020. And the entry-level Xbox? It’ll also run you $600, though you can get a model with less storage for $500. Also having launched in 2020, the lower-storage Xbox Series S used to retail for $300. Both those consoles are more than half a decade old, and they have increased in price by 50 percent.

So what does a consumer do? I’m imagining a guy—perhaps this is you, reader. The last time he bought a console was a PlayStation 3 in 2013 for the previous Grand Theft Auto, or even a PS4 in 2018 for Red Dead Redemption 2. He thought about getting a PS5 for EA Sports College Football in 2024 but ultimately passed.This guy doesn’t pay attention to video games—or he only pays attention to the odd AAA release—and he is expecting to pay $400 for the key to experiencing this generation’s only game that matters. He might not even know that the game itself is going for $80, a rarity that only Nintendo has tried, with Mario Kart World. When he walks up to the counter, this hypothetical man will be paying nearly $900 to be able to play GTA VI. And the worst part? He’s not hypothetical. Starting Thursday, there are going to be thousands of real people in this very situation. Here’s why and what you can do—can you do anything?—about it.

GTA VI has taken over a decade to make (that’s longer than it took Rome to build the Colosseum), and estimates put the budget somewhere north of $1 billion. Rockstar has pushed back, though, with head of publishing Jennifer Kolbe telling Bloomberg that the rumor of the budget surpassing the $1.5 billion budget of the Burj Khalifa is just that—a rumor. Still, it’s not a stretch to call this the biggest media release of all time.

It’s also the biggest test of gaming’s resiliency to price hikes—price hikes that (like everything else, it seems) tie back to AI.

“When [EA Sports College Football] came back a couple of years ago, there was an influx of people who hadn’t bought a console yet,” said Mat Piscatella, a video game industry analyst and advisor at Circana. “Those folks at the time mostly chose either the Xbox Series S or the PlayStation 5 Digital, so the least expensive options to get in.”

The main cause is that the multibillion-dollar gaming industry is in a crisis, a RAM crisis. The RAMageddon, or RAMpocalypse, is a global supply shortage of memory chips that is primarily caused by the post-2024 boom in generative AI. Cleanview, a market-intelligence company, reported that the total number of data centers rose from 361 in 2016 to 1,278 in 2026, with more than five hundred more being built. Data centers are giant warehouses full of computer systems, complex computer systems requiring unprecedented amounts of RAM.

RAM stands for random-access memory. Memory, in this instance, doesn’t mean storage space. RAM is your computer’s “short-term memory,” essentially how many different things your computer’s brain can process at once. The more RAM your computer has, the more it can handle simultaneously. And according to JP Morgan Global Research, prices have risen more than 400 percent in the past two years.

That gets us back to GTA VI and the world of video game consoles. Modern games, with their massive open worlds, highly detailed graphics, and complex physics simulations, require a ton of RAM. As computer parts become scarcer and more expensive, the price of a console goes up. Thus, consoles that—besides the higher-tier PlayStation 5 Pro—have not had meaningful updates in half a decade are now hundreds of dollars more expensive than they were at launch.

Because contrary to what people know about the old gaming business models, the machines themselves still need to turn a profit. It didn’t use to be this way. In the PS2 era, consoles were sold at a loss, with games being the moneymaker. Things changed, audiences grew and moved elsewhere, and—somewhere between microtransactions and Xbox Game Pass—platform holders needed (or preferred) to make money on hardware again. So if a console is more expensive to manufacture, Sony and Microsoft aren’t going to eat that price. Consumers are seeing that increase directly, perhaps more directly and more acutely than we’ve ever seen any increase in technology prices.

Here’s our advice: Don’t wait. The AI push shows no signs of slowing down, and it’s likely that this RAM pipeline bottleneck won’t fix itself for a year or two. This holiday season is getting underway with a supply and demand squeeze similar to what we saw in 2020, when these consoles originally launched.

If you can swing it, you’ll probably want to buy a new console, but the PlayStation 5 Pro is already impossible to find brand-new. It might come down to shopping for renewed and refurbished consoles. These give you a very slight discount, but really they just give you a console that you can plug in and fire up GTA VI on. Welcome to the future of console gaming: refurbished six-year-old consoles.

The beloved activewear brand rarely offers deals like this.
